ParlaMint-SI_2011-07-14-SDZ5-Redna-30.ana.u4	Thank you for your word, Mr. President. Mr. Minister with fellow Members, Members and Members of Parliament! Two fundamental conditions for media freedom and autonomy are the independence and professional competence of journalists and editors and strong civil society. They are based on the social status of journalists and editors, and only a strong civil society can have a decisive impact on the programming content of the media. Only free and autonomous media can realise the public interest that needs to be created in dialogue between all public actors, because the media cannot, and above all must not be the political self-voluntary of every ruling elite or help the interests of capital. However, the experience with the RTV Act has shown that regardless of the quality of the law and the level of dialogue between all actors in the public sphere in the formation of the law, the will of the people in the referendum can be confirmed or rejected. Although the interested and the professional public requested the Media Act, we are once again aware of calls for non-adopt to the law, but also of the public awareness that the law is necessary to improve and modernise the legislation so far, and in particular to comply with the requirements of Directive 2010/13, which fundamentally changes the nature and definition of the media. On the contrary, there is also a threat to this law by referendum and the signatures for it are reportedly already being collected. This is certainly not in favour of the culture of dialogue, but is an attempt to achieve the objective of threats. For it is clear to the not-too-interested co-existence of the legislative procedure that the law from the first to the third reading can be amended and the most controversial provisions can be reformulated by amendments. Although the draft law in the introduction presented public participation in the draft law, which began almost a year ago and continued after the publication of the second draft law in November 2010, the Committee on Culture, Education, Sport and Youth prepared a public presentation of opinions to confront the opinions and views of the professional and interested public after the proposal received government support and was forwarded to the parliamentary procedure. The agreement is broken under individual provisions, but amendments can be prepared for the second reading to address the concerns of our parliamentary group as well as of the professional and interested public. Let me remind you that the Media Act of 2001 and the 2006 novels have also met with a very intense response. The media are so important in shaping our lives that public interest and protection of the public good is the first task of law. Has the law resolved the fundamental dilemmas of journalistic independence and independence? ParlaMint-SI_2011-07-14-SDZ5-Redna-30.ana.u9	Thank you very much. The position of the Parliamentary Group of the Slovenian People's Party will be presented by Janez Ribič.
ParlaMint-SI_2011-07-14-SDZ5-Redna-30.ana.u10	Good afternoon, Mr. Secretary with fellow Members, Members and Members of Parliament! The proposal for a new media law has long been the subject of various debates. The largest and even the most receptive happened two weeks ago right in this hall when there was a public presentation of the opinions on this law. In this public presentation, we have heard very different views, which have largely expressed objections to individual provisions of the law, while rarely anyone has given a general opinion on the law as a whole. In the Parliamentary Group of the Slovenian People's Party we have tried to look at the media law as a whole, what is new, how media autonomy will be ensured, what such a law means for local and regional media, how much of the funds are allocated to the media and, after all, what such a law brings to people, listeners, viewers, readers. We have come to the conclusion that the law establishes a new concept of media concept, which goes largely towards the soft control of the state over the media, and we cannot agree at all in the Slovenian People's Party's Group. In my position, I will focus on some of the content of the law that most of the Slovenian People's Party have fallen into our eyes. First, let me stop at the issue of limited ownership or concentration in the media. Undoubtedly, the concentration of ownership in the media is one of the problems that must be given great attention and be extremely careful in this regard, and above all it is necessary to learn from history. I have in mind in particular the events that revolved around the Pivovarna Laško or the media appetites of its former owners and administrators, who controlled the left share of Slovenian print media by buying the magazines Dela and Evening. Where all this has led to, the birds are already chirping in the branches. Work is facing more than 16 million losses. The proceedings concerning the sale of the watch The evening has been going on for too long, the cargo of both clocks are falling, the employees remain without paid fees and paid social security contributions, etc. The great editorial crisis, due to irregular business decisions, is also faced by the Dnevnik newspaper, where a new responsible editor has been sought for more than a year, and the management of the officer, with the political support of some ruling elites, is still squeezing his head in the sand and does not want to see the obvious. Therefore, in our view, the role of the Office for the Protection of Competition on the issue of media concentration notification should be strengthened, but we do not agree with the proposer that this decision should be under the exclusive competence of the Office. We believe that the current regime must be maintained in order to obtain more than 20% of the ownership or management share in the media, which requires the prior consent of the Ministry of Culture. The regulation granting the Ministry discretion in deciding on the restriction of media concentration is considered appropriate in the Slovenian People's Party's Group, but it should be noted that this procedure should not be extended. In the Slovenian People's Party's Group, we are also concerned about provisions on cross-links in media ownership. We believe that the capital of the largest media owners and those already connected or working in a larger area, such as local or regional, will be again in a privileged position. We estimate that the broadcasters of local and regional radio programmes will be of particular importance under additional impact; they will be impoverished mainly by environments far from the centres, along the borders of Slovenia. The right to information will be deprived of the right to information, the content that is not of interest to capital will be extinguished, the preparation of which is more expensive than the centralised preparation of the news – including the work in the field – and no one will awaken them. Local news, folklore, cultural heritage, language titles, local creativity in the economy, culture, sport, in civil society There will be no opportunities for media literacy of young people, gathering first experiences, opportunities for work in the home environment. [[…]] All these activities are of particular importance to broadcasters of local and regional radio programmes and it is therefore appropriate to consider whether to waive restrictions on cross-ownership under the existing Media Law. We are also skeptical about the provision of Article 95 of the draft law, which reduces the threshold for mandatory notification of media concentration, as this in our view significantly increases the scope of work at the Office for Competition Protection, which is already understaffed in one way or another. In addition, this would interfere too much with the system's competition law, as confirmed by the incumbent of the office of Damjan Matičič. We are convinced that the effects of the proposed regulation on concentration limitation would be virtually null and void, so we do not see any reason for changing it. The next issue, which we pay very close attention to in the Slovenian People's Party, is the appropriate protection of the Slovenian language, which is defined in the third article and also in the section on programme shares. Slovenian language must remain the subject of protection by all media, its role needs to be increased, despite trends in multiculturalism and globalisation, which often devalue Slovenian language in the media space. The Slovenian language is the essence of our identity, our culture, so it must be preserved and its proper use must also be increased in the media, especially in television and radio. The use of the book language is thus more or less a domain of printed media and national radio and television programmes, while in the mass of commercial programmes non-book language forms are increasingly being used, which are in some way a statement of the time we live in, however, must be the responsibility of the media to promote the use of the book language. It is probably not too much to point out that the quality of the expression of young people is falling sharply, mainly due to modern forms of communication, so the responsibility of the media in the language education of young people is even greater. The provision of the third paragraph of Article 62, which implements thematic television programmes which are not subject to the shares of Slovenian audiovisual works, is problematic in our view and does not mean any more than an additional reduction in the importance of Slovenian language in television programmes, which is unacceptable to us. We have a similar view of the deprivation of the right to correction. There is only a possibility of denying published allegations, while the presentation of conflicting facts will be impossible. It should be pointed out that the existing regulation of the right to correction, which also allows for the possibility of showing contradictory facts, is good and in our opinion does not prejudice editorial autonomy. The fact is that, despite the regulation in force, this right is being violated in the media. It is particularly important to take into account professional ethical standards that will prevent the publication of offensive, unverified and untrue indications that clearly violate the dignity of the individual, but the legal norm in this area also gives the weaker parties a legal opportunity to respond and correct. Members of the Slovenian People's Party also object to the creation of a media world, which unfortunately cannot be seen differently as a monitoring political body, which, contrary to the above-mentioned right to correction, constitutes a profound intervention in the autonomy of journalists and editors. There are more facts that make the enforcement of such an authority appear to be harmful to us. First of all, the question of the expertise and apolitical nature of such an authority is raised in the process of its appointment, since the members of the Council are to be appointed by the National Assembly with the majority of the votes of the Members present, which is ridiculous in the short term, given that it is intended to be an autonomous body in a very sensitive area, which is undoubtedly the area of the media. I wonder where such a provision was proposed by those experts who in the recently adopted STA Law wanted to enforce a two-thirds majority of the Members present to appoint members of the Supervisory Board. It is somewhat unusual to approach a very similar issue with diametrically contradictory views. Apparently, these same experts are targeting one of the cities in the world for the media, the path to which it will be much easier. Furthermore, in the Slovenian People's Party, it is estimated that the creation of a media council would open the door to the censorship of journalistic and publicist work, as it will be possible to control the media in a way that will affect, inter alia, the conditions for obtaining the status of independent journalist through the appointment of members of journalists' honorary opinions. This would also affect the freedom of journalistic work and organisation, as all decisions, including the establishment of ethical and professional standards in the media, in the exclusive competence of the world, will be excluded from the process by professional journalists such as the Association of Journalists of Slovenia and the Association of Journalists and Publishers. Then there are provisions on stricter conditions for obtaining the status of a free journalist, which undoubtedly represent a step backwards, as it states here that a candidate should not be convicted at a press hearing, which is somewhat illogical given that most journalists are not involved in any journalistic organisation which cannot therefore be convicted. The provision also opposes Article 42 of the Constitution, which provides for the freedom of association and organisation. There are many more reasons to oppose the establishment of the world, but I will not explain them more. But not to make it look like all the legal provisions are bad, let me say the following. We agree with the provision of the law, which stipulates that the majority of the daily music of Slovenian creators must be played during the afternoon, since so far Slovenian quotas have been met mostly during the nighttime. In our opinion, however, it would be necessary to consider an increase in the percentage of Slovenian music, currently set at 20%, as many Slovenian musicians point out. Also, solutions in the area of greater media pluralisation, especially the possibilities for long-term funding of programmes of public interest, do not seem to us to be so wrong, as they offer more opportunities for the development of local media, especially radio stations, which have already fallen or have been forced to sell their frequencies to non-commercial radio networks. And it was this issue of integration – in the radio network – that was one of the most exposed to the public presentation of opinions, as the proposal of the law shuts down radio networks, which have in recent years, hand to heart, significantly monopolized Slovenian radio space. However, such a drastic measure is excessive and it is impossible to predict what would happen then. Would local radio stations that were forced to join the radio network suddenly begin to create their own program? Very likely not. In the Slovenian People's Party Group, we do not want to put ourselves on the side of radio network owners, who often abuse their superior position, but we need to take a very cautious approach to solving such a problem. In the SLS Group, we find that there are certain solutions in the bill that are urgently needed. In this regard, I have in mind, in particular, the financing of programmes in the public interest, since the law in force does not ensure the survival of local and regional media acting in the public and cultural interest. The current system of financing local media, the rebalancing of the budget of the planned reduction of resources for culture and the monopolisation of media by the largest media outlets, threatens the existence of all the smaller local media, and therefore changes towards greater pluralisation are essential. However, we do not need a new law, but the same effects could be achieved through appropriate amendments to the current law. The fact is that there are many dangers in the proposed law that threaten the freedom of the Slovenian media, so in the SLS Group we will vote against the conclusion that the law is suitable for further consideration, as we estimate that the necessary changes can be achieved already by amending the law in force. We are aware that free and independent media are the foundation of every democratic society, so objective media reporting must become and remain the goal for which we must all, both the media and the public and politics, clearly aspire. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2011-07-14-SDZ5-Redna-30.ana.u12	Mr President, honourable Members and Members of Parliament! The new correction constitutes a means by which the publication of false or false indications is corrected and the right or interest of the person has been affected. The new law removes the prohibition of the incompatibilities of the conduct of telecommunications and radio and television activities and also removes the prohibition of cross-ownership between radio and television programmes. News is also foreseen in the field of media concentration prevention. Following a new assessment of ownership concentration under the exclusive competence of the Competition Protection Office, public interest testing will also be introduced through the new Media Council, and the opinion given by this Council to the parties in the concentration process will not be binding on the Office. Prague, when the buyer of the media has to notify the concentration, is significantly reduced. Professor Pirnat has already given his opinion on this. The next questionable topic is also the ban on setting up radio and television networks or shutting down existing networks. Thus, the law provides, in the transitional and final provisions, that broadcasters of radio or television programmes which will be connected to the television or radio networks when the law is in force must start broadcasting within 1 year at the latest the programme they had before entering the network. Here, too, we had the legal opinion of an expert, in this case Prof. Pirnata, which concludes that such an intervention into the existing situation from a legal point of view is unacceptable for several reasons. In addition, however, there are other reasons that are completely practical or life - style that do not justify such interference with the existing situation. These reasons have already been presented to all of us, in one way or another, and also to Members of the DeSUS, the objections are fully understandable, so the lifting of something, which is successful, which gives bread to many and good media workers and is generally well accepted among a large part of the public, cannot be supported in our Group of Members. ParlaMint-SI_2011-07-14-SDZ5-Redna-30.ana.u16	Thank you so much for saying that, Mr. President. Dear representatives of the government, colleague and colleagues! We are looking at a law that has raised a number of conflicting opinions in the public concerned about the individual solutions that the law brings. That way we have support on the one hand and on the other hand we have total opposition. Since the right to object information is a constitutional category, in the LDS Club we do not want individual solutions in the law to be the result of the narrow interests of certain stakeholders, so our assessment of support for the law will be conditional on solutions that will guarantee the autonomy of the press, transparent ownership of the media, which will not result from the individual interests of holders of political, economic, capital or any other kind of power, and will be protected by public interest in the field of information or media based on autonomy and objectivity. In all the debates and opinions that have been put forward, we also wonder whether this proposal for a law may be disruptive for those who just want the concentration in the field of media to occur, which we do not support and do not want in the LDS. In the LDS, we support the basic objectives of the law: the realization of public interest in the field of media, the provision of greater autonomy to journalists, the definition of the protection of children and adolescents in the media, the transparent regulation of state aid in the field of media, the protection of media pluralism and cultural diversity, better regulation of procedures for the enforcement of the right to correction and response, regulation or provision of an organisational framework for appropriate conditions for the establishment of long-term strategies and policies in the media field, and further support the establishment of effective control over the implementation of legal provisions and the alignment with the EU acquis. This is particularly true of the fact that some of the changes are directly linked to the requirements of the European Union. Of course, in the law, as has been shown by public debate and the type of letters recently, there is still some dilemma, for some also unacceptable solutions, which should be completely contrary to the objectives defined. First of all, the world is exposed here to the media, both its role and powers, as well as the way in which members of the world are appointed, the further acquisition of the status of independent journalist and the permissibility of connections between local radio stations in networks. I will continue to touch only some of the content which, according to the Liberal Democracy Club, have been successfully resolved, as well as the content that is the subject of the dilemma. The bill highlights journalism as a fundamental principle of the Law on Media, which we explicitly support in the LDS Club. Journalist autonomy is ensured in the text of Article 5, which states that the editors, editors, journalists and other authors of contributions, within the framework of the substantive design and in accordance with the basic act of the issuer, are independent and independent in their work. The specific situation of editors and journalists vis-à-vis other employees is based on their special role in society in the exercise of the right to freedom of speech and the right to information, while at the same time the proposal of the law also preserves everyone in the existing Media Act of a specific institute of journalistic autonomy. Next. In view of the regulation so far, the proposal underlines the greater role of the editor in the appointment or discharge of the responsible editor. The managing authority, the issuer, must consult and obtain their opinion on a mandatory basis with the members of the organisation. The approval of the editor in appointing the editor responsible is essential for the administration, as, if not given, he can only appoint the editor responsible by way of the approval of the Supervisory Board. The management is therefore still autonomous in the selection and appointment of the responsible editor, but in this process it must urgently establish a dialogue with the organisation, taking into account its opinion, otherwise the authority responsible for supervision must be included in the procedure. In the LDS Club, we would also like to draw attention to the opinion of Sandra Basić Hrvatin, who believes that the law of this area cannot be fully regulated, since it sets only certain rules and that journalists must choose autonomy on their own. The existing Media Act introduced so-called programmes of special importance which, through budgetary resources, provided content in the public interest; in particular, it involved content or information relevant to the life and work of the local population. Since such a funding method, based on the annual call for proposals, did not provide a long-term solution for radio and television programmes, which in some ways complement the programmes of Radiotelevision of Slovenia, the proposal of the law therefore brings solutions in the form of concessions to those programmes of public and cultural interest. This ensures stable and long-term funding of programmes and ensures public and cultural interest in the field of the media. The proposal for a law in a different way is now governed by a very important right of correction and of reply. This is one of the rights that the Constitution of the Republic of Slovenia places among fundamental human rights and freedoms, which is why special attention must be given to this issue. Therefore, we must clearly answer the question whether the current legal regime has not exceeded the constitutional right to correction and answer and has in some way turned into a tool for pressure on journalists, responsible editors and the media. The bill limits corrections to actual content only. The correction is thus newly defined only as correcting false and false indications in a notice published that would affect the right or interest of that person. We continue to support the provisions in the proposal for a law that also preserves the level of compulsory Slovenian music to 20% of all daily music performances, so that according to the new majority of the Slovenian music quota it must be played during the day. The purpose of the music quotas is primarily to protect and promote Slovenian cultural identity and language, which are subject to both commercial radio and television stations as well as public Radiotelevision Slovenia. Slovenian music or music production of Slovenian creators and creators is important for the preservation of cultural and linguistic identity. In this respect, in our view, the quota is clearly justified and does not constitute an excessive or disproportionate interference with the right to private property of issuers. In the LDS, we particularly welcome the solution that the proposal specifically defines the protection of children and minors in the media as a fundamental principle, since we firmly believe that children and minors benefit from all other rights and judgements. We therefore support the new rule that no child or minor should be subjected to arbitrary and unlawful interference through the media in his private life, family or home, or unlawful attacks against his honour and reputation. There is also a new rule that the publisher in the media content must not use children as a communication tool for the promotion of names or companies, trademarks or images of sponsors, media content in the public. Furthermore, the bill maintains rules on harmful content in print and electronic publications, and also provides child protection in the field of inadequate advertising of unhealthy food and beverages accompanying children's programmes. The bill also provides for the establishment of an independent expert body in the field of media, the media council. The Council is to be composed of distinguished experts from the media field, journalism, communication, telecommunications, information technology, audiovisual culture, law, economics or persons with years of experience in leading or leading media positions. The powers of the world would, in particular, be in the field of ethical, moral and professional standards in the field of media and in monitoring the development of the media landscape. However, as I mentioned at the outset, some opinions on both tasks and competences and on the composition and method of appointing members of the Council are extremely negative and are defined as leverage for the influence of policy on the media. That is why there will certainly be a broader debate on the dilemmas at stake, and we will certainly spend a great deal of time in the debate today. In the LDS, I would like to stress this in particular, we are looking for solutions that would cast any doubt on the desire to reduce the autonomy of journalists and the independence of the media. It also raises a great deal of dust in the text of the bill, by which broadcasters of radio and television programmes are excluded from the possibility of being connected to programme radio or television networks, which has enabled the current Media Act to be connected under certain conditions. In accordance with the proposal of the law, issuers must therefore start broadcasting their programme within 1 year of the entry into force of this Act, in accordance with the initial programming requirements and restrictions set out in the permit to carry out radio or television activities which were in force prior to entry into the network. The reason for abolishing the possibility of networking under specific conditions is solely to abolish an existing regime which, according to the applicants, does not guarantee public interest in the media and which is not consistent with the purpose and purpose of the radio frequency allocation process itself as limited public goods. In doing so, it can be invoked that the broadcasters of the programmes were given frequency in a public call for the dissemination of their programmes in a specific local area, according to which they themselves should provide local interest. Despite the arguments put forward by the applicants for the proposed legal solution, the Liberal Democracy Club also weighs the arguments and messages presented to us by the individual issuers of programmes linked to a particular programme network, and certainly also the arguments presented to us by those who remain independent issuers. At the parliamentary club, we believe that the many solutions offered by the new media law are appropriate and we support them. Of course, we are and will pay attention to certain dilemmas that are still open. Today's first reading of this law is, of course, also understood as an opportunity to respond to a number of these dilemmas in a mutual debate, to seek further solutions to help us improve the text of the media law under consideration in the next legislative procedure. Thank you very much.

Unfortunately, as in all other companies, we have here the phenomenon of speculative ownership, when we don't have interested owners, those who would be behind some media that would take care of this medium, for its development, for its enforcement, but are in the midst of financial speculators, whose parental activity is everything other than the media. As I said at the outset, the law has 132 articles, but it lacks something, perhaps in its final or starting points, in particular it lacks an approach, a responsible approach, which is an approach to dialogue. If two such decisive journalists oppose the law, we should imagine whether it was adopted in the right climate of negotiation or whether it is again one of the laws that will introduce the sui generis solution known from the RTV Act when we mixed public and private. Well, I'll get something else on that. Area of concessions. It's quite a wide area, a lot of articles again. It is not a problem of concession when it comes to the allocation of rights and the implementation of a media-editorial policy in the area of regional, local level; it is a matter of giving business to the private sector. This will always be a controversial area of cross-examination of two motives, two interests and one's fear that public funds will also flow into private interests in this area. The clear definition that this money is intended for the media and media development and that it will be carried out in a non-profit manner creates doubts, doubts already in the public-private partnership system. A private concessionaire works on the principle that he also generates profits, because he probably created himself for it and that is nothing bad, but it is bad that these profits are flowing from the public sphere to the private sphere. In doing so, we all know creative accounting, and even so clear principles and appeals will not solve this dilemma and this law has not saved it. So, the law does not provide key answers to the challenges of today's day and unfortunately after 20 years, we find that we are in a situation where all the merciless entities — journalists' associations — are advertising and criticizing that they are not satisfied with their position; how would we feel today if we heard how many journalists are not receiving social security contributions? There are even more who are not at all in a regular working relationship that is dependent on editors. And if we are talking about freedom of journalism, we should first resolve this area of status, the existence of an individual, because if you depend on whether you're still at work the next day or not, or whether you're going to get a job Most of their contracts are of a temporary nature, and if this status is not resolved if we don't have a journalist who knows that he'll also have the means to survive, we will never talk about free journalism. ParlaMint-SI_2011-07-14-SDZ5-Redna-30.ana.u20	Thank you so much for saying that, Mr. President. Dear representatives of the government, colleagues and colleagues! The media law is generally given a lot of attention in all societies, as its content clearly reflects and affects the democratic nature of society. Given the fact that the impact of the media on political and other social processes is enormous, attention to such laws is even greater. It is also the fact that all media laws face opinions on the basic values of free journalism, on general ethical standards in society and on other more or less important professional matters. We are probably all well aware that this is precisely why there is no absolute truth in relation to the media content and parameters of absolute truth. Very diverse political, professional and other social interests decide to deal with such issues almost without exception. If so, the subjectivity of such factors should be highlighted in particular. In the political as well as the media rather distracted, and to a considerable extent to the dismembered society, as Slovenian is undoubtedly in recent months, the search for a common nominee in the content to be decided by the new media law is all the more so than possible. Our view is that the atmosphere for adopting a politically, professionally and also ethically sensitive and demanding law is, unfortunately, not the most appropriate. We fear that an optimum substantive consensus will be difficult at the acceptance stage. On the contrary, many content will be dominated by the partial interests of one, the other, the third or even the fourth. The question is whether this will be useful from a social and professional point of view. It is also true that the effects of the media on society, in particular the creation of negative stereotypes and their possible abuse, as well as the lack of regulation of the way the media operate and the EU Directorate-General require certain changes or improvements. Many times today, comparisons are made with the disputed Hungarian media law. Some of this has already been mentioned today. The fact is that certain points of contact between the two, that is to say, can be found between Hungary and the proposed Slovenian, but should not be generalised. Nor is it because, following justified pressure from the competent European institutions and the professional and lay public, the Hungarian National Assembly has adopted certain legislative corrections in a positive direction, which they do not report in other countries. Not even in Slovenia. However, it is more important that we learn from Hungarian practice that the adoption of media law requires at least some kind of friendly, political atmosphere for emergency consensus. As was not the case in Hungary with the strong two-thirds majority of the future coalition, I estimate that unfortunately it is not in Slovenia, although for completely different reasons than in Hungary. Of course, it also raises the question of whether social miles can be expected at all, which will be suitable for debates and decisions on sensitive issues of the media law. Probably not, but from now on the more pleasant climate certainly. In the general part of the presentation of our position, without going into the goals, principles and proposed solutions of the law, he could still talk about the eligibility of the Media Council proposal, which is probably the most vulnerable point of such laws. Instead, I'm going to say something that I think is definitely true. The functioning of the media or the environment in which they operate is also very much linked to the state and respect for human rights. At the same time, the media has a major influence on them. It would probably be more difficult to argue that the media are even more important today than ever before, if not the most important factors in the creation of values for all society. In my subjective opinion, we can even place them on the very top of the virtual pyramid of social behaviour and decision-making. That is why the media have special rights, in narration, and at the same time specific obligations and duties which must be clearly determined or in the positive sense of the word regulated. From the point of view of indigenous national communities, the bill summarises certain positive solutions from previous professional and legal practice, but in the case of first-reading support for the law, some corrections or additions will also be needed from our point of view. Both competent umbrella organisations of the Hungarian and Italian national communities duly point out that, for example, in Article 3 of the bill, the protection or character of the Hungarian and Italian languages is too narrow, since the article provides for the use of the name and dissemination of the national media and the publication of their advertising content only as the need or right of indigenous national communities. We must be aware of what the Slovenian Constitution also stipulates, that a broader dimension must be ensured. The language of indigenous national communities, which is an official language in a particular area, is the language of the national mixed area and is intended not only for members of indigenous national communities, but for the territory as such. The law should also ensure the obligation of Slovenian media to a certain amount and level of awareness and information of the population in the Republic of Slovenia with linguistic, cultural, ethnographic and other important values and characteristics of indigenous national communities, since in the case of these, it is a national element. As far as such general Slovenian values are concerned, the draft law sets out them. This weakness is also highlighted in the last report of the competent Commissions of Ministers of the Council of Europe recommending that legislation be upgraded in this direction. The organisations of the two national communities also point to the necessary correction of Article 19 which refers to the conditions for the responsible editor, but it will be necessary to coordinate because there are slightly divergent views and then to have a sound professional opinion on this. Finally, I will raise two more questions that require adequate solutions in the text of the law from the point of view of the national communities. First time. Can the proposed media council, if any, adequately cover any questions and content relating to Hungarian and Italian national media? According to our assessment, by no means at all, so an appropriate solution will have to be found in this regard. Perhaps it would suggest considering the so-called extraordinary or specific members for the needs of the indigenous national media, which, on the basis of a public call, could be appointed by the umbrella organisations of the national communities. They would occasionally be involved by the media in their work, with any disputed issues or other content under their jurisdiction. The second question concerns the proposed article 89 of the legislative text relating to budgetary resources. In view of the actual situation and the practice so far, it will be necessary to lay down in the aforementioned Article of the Act that the implementation of the RTV programmes of Italian and Hungarian nationality will undoubtedly require budgetary resources in a certain proportion, as these are specific programmes which form an integral part of the programmes in the public and cultural interest of the Republic of Slovenia. Since it has always been stuck in the past in determining or providing a budget share, it would also suggest that this aspect should be regulated more consistently. If the laws are being renewed or a new law is being passed, the areas to be regulated should also be substantively upgraded. So if the bill is supported and proposed for a second reading, we also look forward to its substantive upgrade from the point of view of the national communities. In that case, the law will receive our support. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2011-07-14-SDZ5-Redna-30.ana.u44	Thank you very much for your words, Mr. Vice President! Now that we are addressing this subject, in particular the question of the exercise of the right to correction and the answer, I recall how, in our great enthusiasm 20 years ago, we designed this chapter in the Constitution, which deals with human rights and fundamental freedoms. And then we introduced a number of human rights into a particular way that we cannot find a proper comparison in other countries and in other constitutions today. Nevertheless, I am convinced that it was the same and that it is right to strive to implement as high standards as possible in the field of the protection of human rights and fundamental freedoms. And the right to correction and the right to reply under our constitution are not classic human rights. While the Constitution does not explicitly provide for a legal reservation under these two rights, it follows from their nature that they cannot be exercised directly on the basis of the Constitution, but that the way in which they are implemented must be prescribed by law. There is nothing we can do to help by comparing the way in which both rights are exercised in other democratic countries. The language interpretation of these arrangements leads us to conclude that there is no correction in these corrections, but an answer; a risk in Italy, for example, and a response in France. And that answer Ali is just that answer, like I said. [[…]] The answer is also not an answer, but a legal institute, which is best labeled as a complement to information. The former Judge of the Constitutional Court, Franz Testen, in his comment on Article 40 of the Constitution, argues that the right to correction is granted in the private interest of the individual concerned. However, the right to reply is given in the public interest, and it also states that it does not show that it is too far-reaching and, in particular, not reliable decisions. Nor can we seek appropriate answers in the case-law of the European Court of Human Rights, since the European Convention on Human Rights does not know the right to correction and/or the answer. Some argue that the right to correction is in conjunction with the right to personal dignity and the protection of privacy and personal rights. The right of reply should be linked to the right of everyone to receive information of a public nature. In short, matter is very complicated. There are no uniform answers as to how the most right and in accordance with our Constitution would be to regulate in the media law the way in which both rights are exercised. This should also take into account the genetics of how and what has happened in Article 40 of the Constitution. Surely the reasons and reasons in the media practice in the system before 1990 concern. Personally, I think it is important that this law, which we are dealing with today, does not reduce the level of protection of the right to correction and the response that has been established so far. Therefore, in itself, there is some doubt as to the appropriateness or appropriateness of the proposed regulation in Article 39, which somehow allows for the opinion of the responsible editor as to whether it is at all an answer which is in the public interest. Such an explanation has also been allowed, and I see that many journalists have adopted such an explanation. The interests of the responsible editor and the interest of the beneficiary are, as we know, generally in conflict, and this issue must be addressed in particular in the further drafting process of this law. I must be so honest that in this place I say that, in the previous term, we warned the opposition about the regulation of the right to correction and the answer that is in the current law, that the government policy will certainly abuse it. Today, most of all, I think that our fears have been exaggerated and unjustifiable. Therefore, I also hope that we have all reached a stage where it is in the interest of all of us to regulate the way these rights are exercised so that the rights are best protected and that we will ensure the standards already achieved. I also wonder, for example, whether all such detailed procedural provisions are necessary in the event of an action and proceedings before a court. Are the general rules of the civil court not sufficient? Nevertheless, I believe that in the next legislative procedure mistakes and concerns raised so far by the public debate can be addressed. There are certain solutions that must be further explored and thought out, and I will therefore support this bill's proposal. But I'm sure they mean the basis for us to get to a good marriage. Thank you very much.

The bill repeals the prohibition of incompatibilities in the conduct of telecommunications and radio and television activities. It repeals the prohibition of cross-ownership and the prohibition of broadcasting between radio and television programmes. It lifts the absolute ban on connections – ownership, publishing, connected persons and the like, between general information logs or weeklyes and radio or television programmes. It is therefore a matter of greater liberalisation of the ownership of the media market. The law justifies insufficient regulation of ownership relations, insufficient regulation of ownership and insufficient regulation of capital acquisitions or concentration of ownership in media companies. Instead of enhanced regulation and strict rules restricting media owners, the law brings greater liberalisation, which according to the organisation of journalists will allow for imprudent ownership and financial transactions, allowing the entry into the media of speculators and owners who would like to exhaust the media only financially or use them for political purposes. Nor does the bill protect adequately acquired rights of the population to a constitutionally guaranteed response to false or malicious charges in the media. We've heard about this before. The right to correction and reply is one of the rights that the Constitution of the Republic of Slovenia places on fundamental human rights and freedoms. The proposed law considers that the current right of residents to correction and reply, which means for the issuer to publish certain content, may constitute an intervention in the economic interest of the issuer and as a restriction on the right of the media or editors to decide on the publication. This puts the drafter of the law on the economic interest of the issuer and the interests of the editors on the right to reply to false statements in the media. This is a decline in the already achieved standard, which cannot be agreed on. The key purpose of this law is that the assets of those subject to criminal prosecution – but this prosecution is only an opportunity to launch a financial investigation into their alleged illegal assets – continue this financial investigation separately, independently and can lead to a final finding of illegal property, which also means the final seizure of this property. It is a law that wants to make a key contribution to the fight against organised crime, including, in particular, economic crime. ParlaMint-SI_2011-07-14-SDZ5-Redna-30.ana.u73	Thank you very much. The position of the Government will be explained by the State Secretary at the Ministry of Justice, Mr Boštjan Škrlec.
ParlaMint-SI_2011-07-14-SDZ5-Redna-30.ana.u74	Thank you very much, Mr. Vice President. Dear Members and Members, welcome! The basic purpose and purpose of the bill is to prevent the acquisition and use of assets of illicit origin. Such property should, of course, be taken away from the persons with whom they have it, with the protection guaranteed of their rights and the rights of those persons who have acquired such property lawfully and voluntarily. The proposed regime aims to ensure the confiscation of assets of illicit origin in a special procedure which, in criminal proceedings, has only a starting point in terms of suspicion of illegal origin of property and the suspicion of service of one of the catalogue-defined offences. The substantive basis for the confiscation procedure shall be provided in the financial investigation provided for in this proposal. The suspension shall be decided at the first stage by a criminal court responsible for the prosecution of a suspect of a catalytical crime service, and then by a court of justice which shall also be responsible for the final confiscation decision. In doing so, the principle of reverse evidentiary burden is laid down, which means that the legality of the origin of that property, which exceeds the value legally expressed, lies with the owner or beneficiary of such property. The Government of the Republic of Slovenia supports the legitimate and legitimate objective pursued by the proposal of this law, namely that no one can retain the assets acquired in an illegal way or by illegal activity. In the event of the establishment of a suspicion of criminal service, one of the most serious criminal offences that are specified by the catalogue, the system of verification of the origin of such property is in place. The procedure for the confiscation of assets of illicit origin under this law is therefore not a criminal or criminal procedure, but rather a civil proceeding as a result of the fact that the perpetrator or other beneficiary has acquired property by an unlawful act. In doing so, the Government of the Republic of Slovenia estimates that the Members have also taken into account some of the concerns raised by the previous expert debate on the inadequacy and ambiguity of the text previously proposed. Furthermore, we are extremely positive about the fact that the bill does not introduce new state authorities, but imposes powers and tasks under this law on the state authorities that are already in operation and within their existing areas of operation. This ensures proper respect for the principle of economics and the cost-effectiveness of public spending. There are still certain concerns in the legal section regarding this text of the proposal, in particular regarding the proportionality of the measures envisaged and the fact that the seizure of illegally acquired property does not require a criminal conviction. Given that it is a whole new institution in the Slovenian legal order, a broad professional debate is particularly welcome, as it allows for reflection and, as a result, improvements in the text can also be made. The Government of the Republic of Slovenia supports the proposal of the law and considers it suitable for further consideration. The legislative procedure will, however, need to give due consideration to all the warnings of the legal profession and, where appropriate, to complete the text with appropriate amendments. Thank you very much.

If the authorities take it away from me and return it to the right owner, they shall not violate my constitutionally protected right to property. ParlaMint-SI_2011-07-14-SDZ5-Redna-30.ana.u84	Thank you so much for saying that. Dear Mr Vice-President, distinguished Mr Secretary of State, distinguished colleagues and colleagues! The proposal provides for a compromise solution, namely a special procedure which has only a basis in criminal proceedings in view of the suspicion of illegal origin of property. The temporary insurance itself shall be withdrawn and the final withdrawal shall be decided by the court in a fair proceeding. It provides for the principle of the reverse burden of proof if there is an apparent imbalance between the legitimate income and the value of the property, which means that it will be on the side of the person concerned or the property of the suspect origin to prove how it has come to this property. ParlaMint-SI_2011-07-14-SDZ5-Redna-30.ana.u88	Thank you so much for saying that, Mr. President. Mr Secretary of State with co-workers, colleagues and colleagues! In the LDS Club, we are convinced that the Republic of Slovenia must be fully clear and universally accepted and that the belief that dishonesty and infringement of the rules should not be allowed to be rewarded. All those who have seized property in an illegal way must be put on their fingers. Not only by more effective prosecution of crimes in the system, which, by amending the Law on Judicial Service and the Law on Public Prosecutions, is becoming more modern and better prepared for the challenges posed earlier by criminal associations and individuals, but also by the effective seizure of that property resulting from illegal acts. At the LDS, we estimate that a set of legislative changes in this area is prepared comprehensively and imaginatively. The establishment of the National Investigation Office, the establishment of specialised departments in the courts and the prosecution, and the clear priorities for the prosecution of organised and economic crime prove this too. We cannot and will not accept that the property of illegal origin accumulates among individuals who, at the expense of long procedures and skilled lawyers, either hide or never lose such property because the process of proving the illegality of the origin of property is extremely difficult under today's legislation. The issue of lawfully or illegally acquired property has become, above all, a pressing issue in our society in recent years. In the LDS, we believe that, in the field of detection, temporary insurance, temporary confiscation and confiscation of assets of illicit origin, a different regulatory framework should be established from the current one, and therefore we support the solutions brought by the law on the confiscation of assets of illegal origin. The disproportionateity between income and property of anyone is a sign that something is simply wrong. The key leap proposed by this law is that it gives the State the opportunity to demand from an individual, on the basis of reasonable suspicions and evidence gathered, to prove the origin of its assets. The laws must apply equally, while the State must regulate the subsystems by protecting both the public interest and the rights of individuals. We cannot claim that the laws apply equally if, on the one hand, the State is able to recover unpaid offences or taxes from many citizens, and on the other hand, it cannot confiscate property for all those who have acquired their property in an illegal way. At the LDS Club, we are convinced that there must be no double criterion that we are all equal before the law, but in particular that laws must not allow exceptions or that such a marriage hole must be corrected immediately and inconsistently eliminated. In the drafting of the law on the confiscation of property of illegal origin, we believe that the interests of the State, politics and people are united. This has also been shown in the preparation of this legislation when one draft law was prepared in the first phase and then, in cooperation with a large number of political parties, the interested public and also the Ministry of Justice, a proposal for a law that we are considering today. The proposal of the law, as the basic objective for which we fully agree in the LDS Club, lays down the prevention of the acquisition and use of assets of illicit origin. Such property must be taken away, and it is also important to protect the rights of persons who have acquired their property lawfully and voluntarily. It is also crucial to understand the law that this law does not bring about the inquisition or the pursuit of property in general. The provisions of the law clearly lay down the conditions which must be met so that the financial investigation procedure, which is essential for the initiation of the confiscation procedure of assets of illicit origin, may begin at all. The financial investigation, which is limited to a maximum of one year, by a possible extension, shall be ordered by the State Prosecutor, provided that the pre-trial or criminal proceedings establish grounds for suspecting that the suspect has committed a catalytic offence and has owned, owned, used or consumed assets worth more than EUR 50 000 in respect of which there are grounds for suspecting that he or she had acquired or transferred such assets to his or her legal successors or transferred them to related persons or was mixed with the assets of those persons. This means that in addition to the suspect, the legality of the property will also have to be demonstrated by the suspects' close relatives, to whom the property has also been transferred. There will be no more concealment of property at the expense of family ties, and so is the case. However, the key procedural news brought by the law follows when or if the financial investigation finds the facts and provides evidence that gives the State Prosecutor of the specialised state prosecutor of the Republic of Slovenia, established under the new State Prosecutor's Act, the basis for bringing an action against the owner of the property. The procedure or action for the confiscation of property of illegal origin is a fair and not a criminal proceeding as has been the case so far. It is precisely because the confiscation process is not a criminal procedure and it is against the property of illegal origin and because the act by which the property was acquired has been unlawfully at the time of acquisition that we cannot discuss the infringement of the presumption of innocence and the violation of the constitutional right to property. The case-law of the European Court of Human Rights has shown that adapting the actions of countries to the development of organised crime does not automatically violate human rights, which some lawyers and legal theorists fear. The measures that follow the legitimate interest of the State and at the same time ensure the relative protection of the individual are not in violation of the rights of the European Convention on Human Rights. Second, and this is a key novelty brought by the law under consideration, is the introduction and application of the institute of reverse burden of proof in a fair trial. This means that the defendant must challenge the presumption of unlawfully acquired property by proving that the property was obtained from legitimate sources. In the LDS, we believe that these two key innovations and, of course, the whole process, as foreseen by the law, are of paramount importance for the identification and disposal of assets of illegal origin to be significantly faster and more effective in the future. At the same time, we regret that the relevant legal solutions for this area were not adopted years ago. Despite all of this, it is never too late for the mental leap that the law under consideration brings about in the area of the seizure of illegally acquired property. In Slovenia, the principle of honesty is to be strengthened and I am sure that most Slovenes and Slovenes live in accordance with this principle. But it is up to us to consolidate this principle in society with appropriate legislative changes and to put our finger on those who are scolding at the rules and the rule of law. This law is a step in this direction. At the LDS, we are pleased that the drafting of the text of the law has led to a broader debate and a confrontation of different opinions, as reflected in the drafting of the new text of the law, which has largely taken into account the comments and proposals that have emerged in these debates. We are also joining the dilemmas about the time frame within which the procedure should be followed – today the law defines five years – so we ourselves think that there is still a need for further reflection and possible correction of this period to possible longer time reflections. However, we certainly believe that this is a decision that must be weighed and have its basis in professional arguments. In the LDS, we estimate that the law is in the proposed form suitable for further consideration, but we also clearly express the willingness to cooperate in its upgrading and efforts to finally adopt and enter into force. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2011-07-14-SDZ5-Redna-30.ana.u91	Thank you very much. The position of the Group of Social Democrats will be presented by Anton Kolarič. Please.
ParlaMint-SI_2011-07-14-SDZ5-Redna-30.ana.u92	Thank you very much, Mr. President. Representative of the Government, colleagues and colleagues. The economic and financial crisis, as well as the examples in the neighbouring countries in which media-responsible actions of property confiscation took place last year, have also led us to believe that a group of Members and Members have put the proposed law on the confiscation of property of illegal origin into the legislative process. The proposal received immediate high political support, and on the other hand there were some opponents or critics of the proposed text. They felt that such legislation was unnecessary, since such anomalies were supposed to be removed in criminal law already – an established principle that no one should retain the property benefits derived from the crime. However, this institute has not worked or has not yet worked, at least not to the extent that we all want together. In February this year, a public presentation of opinions was made in the National Assembly, where representatives of various institutions dealing with related issues welcomed any solutions that will start to regulate this area, and in the final stage led to the thought that the crimes on the basis of which an individual or a association has benefited are not being paid. It turned out that the original text of the bill was not well thought out. On the basis of comments and opinions, a new draft law has thus been drawn up, which we consider appropriate in the Group of Social Democrats for further consideration in order to be adopted as soon as possible. And what does the proposal of marriage actually bring? At the level of the general provision, the purpose of the bill is defined by defining the public interest which justifies an intervention in the constitutionally protected right to property by the fact that a suspect has acquired property by means of an illegal act on the basis of a catalogue of that offence or another non-believer. The act by which the property was acquired was illegal at the time of acquisition and therefore the right of ownership could not have been legally created. The assets of illegal origin are defined as assets of a suspect, accused person, convicted person or person suspected of having committed an offence, and in respect of property it has not been proven to have been derived from his legal income. However, the introductory provisions also establish assumptions in respect of which evidence will be provided in the proceedings for the confiscation of assets of illegal origin. Thus, it is assumed that the assets were not derived from legitimate income if the apparent imbalance between its volume and its income reduced by taxes and contributions paid by those persons, suspects, accused persons or the fugitive during the period in which the property was acquired was made. Secondly, that the assets of illicit origin have been transferred free of charge or for a payment which does not correspond to the actual value if they have been transferred to a closely connected person or family member. Of course, both assumptions can be challenged, as shown in the section on the procedure for the confiscation of assets of illegal origin. Given the nature of the individual stages of the procedure under this proposal of the law, there is a reasonable application of other rules unless otherwise specified by this proposal, which will facilitate the application of the law in practice. An important news is a financial investigation. It shall be designated by the State Prosecutor if the prosecution or criminal proceedings establish grounds for suspecting that the suspect has committed a catalogue crime. The financial investigation shall take place for a period as recorded five years before the year in which it was introduced. If, during the financial investigation, there are grounds for believing that the assets have been transferred or transferred to related persons, the State Prosecutor shall order that the financial investigation also be extended against those persons. By the way, regarding five years, it may still be a matter of discussion and agreement in the next stages of marriage. The procedure lays down the conditions for temporary confiscation insurance and for the temporary confiscation of assets of illicit origin. It also provides for a procedure for the confiscation of assets of illegal origin. The procedure for the confiscation of property of illegal origin under this law is a civil act and is not linked to a legally terminated criminal procedure. The defendant may challenge the presumption that he has acquired property due to any catalytic crime, which means that he must prove that he has paid actual value in the acquisition of the property. And the law also mentions the catalogue offences under the Penal Code, which are generally related to organised criminal activity, the purpose of which is to acquire property of illegal origin. These are serious crimes such as terrorism, slavery, trafficking in human beings, unjustifiable production and possession of drugs, unjustifiable production and trafficking in illicit drugs, illicit substances in sport, the provision of illicit drugs, the organisation of money chains and illicit gambling, offences against the economy, taking and giving bribes, etc., until the last indent – other conceivable offences for which five years of imprisonment may be punishable or more if they may result in assets of illegal origin. The proposed law does not impose new public authorities, but imposes new tasks and powers on the State Prosecutor's Office, the police, customs, tax administration, the Anti-Money Laundering Office, to follow the basic principle of the law, namely that no one can retain the assets acquired in an illegal way or by illegal activity. Let me finish. When we introduced the law at the press conference, I said the law was too late, but I hope it wasn't too late. Since Roman times, the thought has been preserved that the perpetrator must return the matter or its value to which it came in an unlawful way. Maybe we've forgotten a little about this in our time. Today it has been said that we may not be serious about the signatories. I must say that the Social Democrats and the law are certainly serious, we are ready for further talks in the next stages, and we hope that we will be able to support it together as soon as possible. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2011-07-14-SDZ5-Redna-30.ana.u100	Excuse me, Mr President, I respect what you are doing as President of the National Assembly in conducting this procedure. The law we are dealing with today is a very, more specifically, a very serious thing, so to speak, a dramaticly serious thing. That's how I take it. This new approach – the seizure of assets of illegal origin, without first proving to the perpetrator the service of a criminal offence, but it is a confiscation of property that is not proven by its owner and corresponds to the service of a criminal offence – still raises a number of serious questions in criminal law theory. Indeed, the current social situation and the many anomalies in society, which we also see in us every day, require some new defence mechanisms of society and require new ways of effectively combating illegal, illegal acquisition of property, mostly abnormally high values. Indeed, a number of international instruments have introduced certain elements and grounds for resolving these issues, but rather for the legal regulation of the entire mechanism. Let me just say: the Civil Law Convention on Corruption, the Criminal Law Convention on Corruption, the United Nations Convention against the Illicit Traffic in Drugs, the United Nations Convention against International Organized Crime, the International Convention on the Suppression of the Financing of Terrorism, the Convention, simplified if I say so, on money laundering and so on. In short, especially after 11 September 2001, such international treaties are more relaxed. ParlaMint-SI_2011-07-14-SDZ5-Redna-30.ana.u148	Thank you very much, Mr. Vice President. I would like to address two important issues that have been raised in the debate. One relates to the fulfilment of the international obligations of the Republic of Slovenia, i.e. ratified international conventions, and the already mentioned reservation to the Convention on the Prevention of Money Laundering and the Financing of Terrorism. It was a reservation regarding the establishment of the institute of reverse burden of proof, which was also logical, since at the time when Slovenia ratified this Convention, it did not even have this issue regulated in national law, not in the field of criminal procedure and criminal law at all, and it was not clear at the time what concept of the law on the confiscation of property of illegal origin Slovenia would decide. I would like to remind you of the debate that took place here in this Chamber of the National Assembly in the public presentation of opinions on the institution of the confiscation of property of illegal origin. There, in the discussions of various experts, there were very different proposals for what model and what concept Slovenia should decide. This is not even appropriate at this time to list all these models, which are also comparatively well-known, but one of them, of course, is also one of the possibilities for Slovenia to decide on the principle of reverse burden of proof in the context of criminal proceedings. We are here today discussing the law decided by the Group of Members and supported by the Government, which speaks of the reverse burden of proof in the civil procedure. That's the difference I'd like to point out. This realization that this procedure can be more effective than a criminal procedure has also been reached by the applicants, and the Government agrees and supports this position. This reservation to the Convention is in no way an obstacle to the adoption of this law. On the contrary, when this law is obviously adopted in the National Assembly, given that a large majority of the groups have announced support for it, Slovenia will then be able to formally withdraw the already mentioned reservation to the Council of Europe Convention. This issue does not in any way impede and has not in the past impeded this issue of the preparation of an appropriate regulation to deal with the issue of the confiscation of property of illegal origin. However, the second issue raised in the debates concerns the period already mentioned, for five years, where different interpretations and understandings appear clearly from the point of view of the effectiveness of this law. Here I would like to point out that the Government understands this provision, which is now the subject of a legislative procedure, so that this period of five years therefore refers to a period of five years of possession of this property, for which there is a discrepancy between its scope and its origin, which in fact means that this property could have been acquired substantially early, so 20 years ago can also be. It is important that the availability during this five-year period of such assets has been made available, and therefore there should be no major difficulties in this regard in practice. We have seen another similarity in this five-year period, i.e. the same five-year period also applies to the area of taxes and tax rules implemented by the competent tax authorities, and here the drafter has clearly followed established practice in this area. This also means, according to the Government's assessment, that efficiency in the sense of implementing all the provisions of the law should in no case be affected. Finally, let me point out once again that this law is extremely important, as many Members have discussed today, and it is, of course, part of a larger whole, which relates to major changes in the field of our criminal justice and which you have already decided in one part when you have adopted the Law on the State Prosecutor's Office, and in one part I hope that you will still be when you decide on the amendment of the Penal Code and the Law on Criminal Procedure. This is a series of links between all these provisions. With regard to the already adopted State Prosecutor's Act, this is an important connection because the newly established 12 specialized prosecutor's office will be the one that will conduct all proceedings relating to the seizure or confiscation of assets of illegal origin and which will also lead this institution of financial investigation, and therefore this new prosecutor's office in the adopted Public Prosecutor's Act provides for a wide range of powers and possibilities for cooperation with other authorities – from the tax authorities to the Office for the Prevention of Money Laundering, Customs, etc. On the other hand, it is an important connection with the Penal Code and the amendment of the Penal Code, because this law on the confiscation of property of illicit origin is, of course, based on certain suspicions that the specified catalogue-specific offences are committed. These are, of course, being recast in one part by the already mentioned amendment of the Penal Code, and this is, of course, important in terms of the effectiveness of the application and implementation of the provisions of the law on the confiscation of property of illegal origin. Finally, it is also related to the amendment of the Criminal Procedure Act, again on the one hand, to those provisions relating to the seizure or confiscation of property acquired or derived from a criminal offence and, on the other hand, to the powers of state prosecutors, who in one piece are greatly expanded in criminal proceedings and give them great opportunity to be more successful in the pursuit of economic crime in particular. Therefore, the Government considers it extremely important that all these laws, at the head of what we are currently dealing with, thus the law on the confiscation of assets of illegal origin, are further adopted. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2011-07-14-SDZ5-Redna-30.ana.u150	Thank you very much, Mr Vice President, for the floor. Ladies and gentlemen of the House! The preparation of the new Penal Code is one of the most important projects in the entire mandate of this government – as defined by this government – among the projects related to the fight against economic crime, which is linked to the amendment of the Criminal Procedure Act and the State Prosecutor's Office Act, and a novelty in the area of the seizure of assets of illegal origin has been addressed. The main reasons for preparing amendments to the Penal Code are actually three. Firstly, these are systemic changes, which were identified as necessary by the Slovenian criminal law theory at the time of the preparation and implementation of this Penal Code. Secondly, it is to take into account the warnings and proposals of the competent judicial authorities, in particular the prosecutions and the courts, as regards inconsistencies, shortcomings detected in the application of the Penal Code. Thirdly, we urgently need to carry out appropriate harmonisations and adjustments, especially in the specific part of the Penal Code, with amended grounds for the legal order of the European Union and, of course, with binding international instruments, which the Republic of Slovenia is obliged to enforce in an appropriate manner in its internal legal order. The scope of these changes is limited. It is limited only to the most important corrections to which it has warned the profession and which are important for the effective functioning of criminal justice. This does not mean that any other provision in the current Penal Code would not merit further analysis and an assessment of the need to amend, at the head of, for example, the Institute of Life imprisonment. The main changes in the amendment go in two directions. One direction relates to the general part of the Penal Code. This must be the most important change in the arrangements for compulsory psychiatric treatment. This novelty, in line with the majority position of the profession, re-regulations the procedure for carrying out this compulsory psychiatric treatment in the institution and in the free for unaccounted-for offenders in the context of criminal proceedings. Other major changes under this general section of the Penal Code are, in particular, the regulation of certain institutions, such as: extreme force, provisions on counterfeiting and negligence, then provisions on the daily amounts of fines – The Government wants to apply more than ever in practice the provisions of the law relating to the imposition of fines – further the issue of the imposition of penalties for offences in bulk, and the establishment of new grounds for the mitigation of penalties, the establishment of grounds for the confiscation of property benefits resulting from the criminal offence, and the modification of the way in which prison sentences are replaced, as well as some others. In the specific part of the Penal Code, which is the second set of important amendments, it is therefore intended to harmonise with international legal acts in certain important crimes, including those relating, for example, to sexual inviolability, and in crimes against the environment and space, natural goods, black employment, public promotion of hatred, violence and so on. Perhaps the most important changes in this specific part of the Penal Code relate to crimes against the economy. It is about substantially changing some institutions, such as: false bankruptcy, fraud in obtaining and using loans or benefits, abuse of position or trust in economic activity, tax evasion, definition, that is, definition of economic activity in general work, and of course a new crime, compensation of public funds. This crime is extremely important and has arisen in close cooperation with the Court of Auditors, which has identified a number of systemic problems that this Penal Code solves through its audits of the legality and legality of operations in the country. Finally, let me point out that the following is another important change that we are already preparing and already in professional coordination, which is a criminal law that will regulate the criminal responsibility of minors.
